assistant: ### 5.1 Problem Statement
The DatePicker component has three main issues:
1. **Inaccessible internal widget**: The internal `date_picker` widget is not exposed as a public attribute
2. **Layout kwargs not handled**: The `layout_kwargs` parameter is passed but not processed to set layout properties
3. **Date picker kwargs not passed**: Parameters like `min` and `max` are not passed to the internal date_picker widget

### 5.2 Problem Location
The problem is in `/testbed/sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/inputs.py` in the `DatePicker` class constructor (lines 51-90).

### 5.3 Test Reproduction
The failing test `test_kwargs` in `/testbed/tests/test_DatePicker.py` reproduces the issue by:
1. Passing date picker kwargs (`min`, `max`) and expecting them to be accessible via `datepicker.date_picker.min/max`
2. Passing `layout_kwargs` and expecting layout properties to be set correctly

### 5.4 Best Practices
- Expose internal components when they need to be accessible for customization
- Properly handle parameter separation (layout vs component specific)
- Maintain backward compatibility
- Follow consistent patterns used by other widgets in the codebase

### 5.5 Solution Approach
1. **Expose internal date_picker**: Store the created `date_picker` widget as `self.date_picker`
2. **Handle layout_kwargs**: Process the `layout_kwargs` parameter to set layout properties
3. **Pass date picker kwargs**: Separate date picker specific kwargs and pass them to the internal widget
4. **Preserve v_model handling**: Ensure default v_model values work correctly

    53	       <#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int stride = blockDim.x;

    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    extern __shared__ float sdata[];
    float* max_shared = sdata;
    float* sum_shared = &sdata[stride];

    // Find max value using thread-local variable first
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }

    // Store thread-local max in shared memory
    max_shared[tid] = thread_max;
    __syncthreads();

    // Parallel reduction for max
    for (int offset = stride/2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {
        if (tid < offset) {
            max_shared[tid] = max(max_shared[tid], max_shared[tid + offset]);
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float max_val = max_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Compute exponentials and partial sums in thread-local variable
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;  // Store intermediate result
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }

    // Store thread-local sum
    sum_shared[tid] = thread_sum;
    __syncthreads();

    // Parallel reduction for sum
    for (int offset = stride/2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {
        if (tid < offset) {
            sum_shared[tid] += sum_shared[tid + offset];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float sum_val = sum_shared[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;

    // Final normalization without atomic operations
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim((batch_size + THREADS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / THREADS_PER_BLOCK);

    // Double the shared memory to store both max and sum values
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

So again, each a_i has to be at least 5, but the sum of the a_i's is 15. Wait, but if you consider l^p space for p between 1 and infinity, does the maximum |α_i| get larger?

#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

// CUDA kernel for softmax using stride loops and proper boundary handling
__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    // Each block processes one row of the input (batch element)
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int blockSize = blockDim.x;

    // Pointers to the start of the current row
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    // Allocate shared memory: first half for max reduction, second half for sum reduction
    extern __shared__ float shared_mem[];
    float* max_shared = shared_mem;
    float* sum_shared = shared_mem + blockSize;

    // Step 1: Compute the maximum value in the row using stride loop
    float max_val_local = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        float val = x_row[i];
        if (val > max_val_local) {
            max_val_local = val;
        }
    }
    max_shared[tid] = max_val_local;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the maximum value in shared memory
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            if (max_shared[tid + s] > max_shared[tid]) {
                max_shared[tid] = max_shared[tid + s];
            }
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float row_max = max_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 2: Compute exponentials and partial sum using stride loop
    float sum_val_local = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - row_max);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;  // Store the intermediate exponential result
        sum_val_local += exp_val;
    }
    sum_shared[tid] = sum_val_local;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the sum of exponentials
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            sum_shared[tid] += sum_shared[tid + s];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float total_sum = sum_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 3: Normalize the results
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        y_row[i] = y_row[i] / total_sum;
    }
}

// CUDA forward function
void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);

    // Allocate shared memory for both max and sum reductions
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

// C++ forward function exposed to PyTorch
torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    int batch_size = x.size(0);
    int num_features = x.size(1);

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(x.data_ptr<float>(), y.data_ptr<float>(), batch_size, num_features);

    return y;
}

// Pybind11 module
PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");
}

   5.4 State clearly the best practices to take into account<#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256
#define WARP_SIZE 32

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val = max(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));
    }
    return val;
}

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    }
    return val;
}

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int lane_id = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;

    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    extern __shared__ float shared[];
    float* warp_maxes = shared;
    float* warp_sums = &shared[num_warps];

    // Find max value using thread-local variable first
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }

    // Warp-level reduction for max
    float warp_max = warp_reduce_max(thread_max);

    // Store warp results in shared memory
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_maxes[warp_id] = warp_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < num_warps) {
        float final_max = warp_maxes[lane_id];
        final_max = warp_reduce_max(final_max);
        warp_maxes[0] = final_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    float max_val = warp_maxes[0];

    // Compute exponentials and partial sums
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }

    // Warp-level reduction for sum
    float warp_sum = warp_reduce_sum(thread_sum);

    // Store warp results
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_sums[warp_id] = warp_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < num_warps) {
        float final_sum = warp_sums[lane_id];
        final_sum = warp_reduce_sum(final_sum);
        warp_sums[0] = final_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    float sum_val = warp_sums[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;

    // Final normalization
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);

    int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * num_warps * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

So, if we can choose the norms of the vectors e_i, then we can make 5 / ||e_i|| as small as we like, making the sum |a₁| + |a₂| + |a₃| as small as we like. But the problem states that the vectors are fixed in the Banach space. Wait, the problem says "Consider a real Banach space E and n linearly independent vectors e₁, e₂, …, eₙ ∈ E." So, the vectors are given, but their norms are not specified. Since the answer must hold for any such Banach space and any such vectors, we have to consider the worst case scenario where the sum is minimized.

Wait, but this is getting too convoluted. Let me think of it in terms of linear functionals. For each i, |α_i| is bounded by ||f_i|| * ||v||, where v is the vector and f_i is the coordinate functional. So, if ||v|| ≤5, then |α_i| ≤5||f_i||. Therefore, |a_i| must be at least 5||f_i||. The sum |a₁| + |a₂| + |a₃| is 5(||f₁|| + ||f₂|| + ||f₃||). To minimize this sum, we need to minimize the sum of the norms of the coordinate functionals. 

But in a 3-dimensional Banach space, for any basis, the sum of the norms of the dual basis satisfies some inequalities. I recall that in finite-dimensional spaces, there's a relationship between the basis and the dual basis. For example, if the original basis is normalized (each e_i has norm 1), then the dual basis vectors f_i have norms at least 1, but the sum can vary. In the case of a Hilbert space with orthonormal basis, the dual basis is the same as the original, so each f_i has norm 1, and the sum is 3. But if the original basis is not orthogonal, the dual basis norms can be larger. However, since the problem doesn't specify the basis or the space, perhaps the minimal sum is 15, achieved when the basis is orthonormal in a Hilbert space, and since in other spaces the sum can't be lower, the minimal possible value is 15.

Alternatively, maybe the minimal sum is 5, but that seems too low. If each a_i=5, sum is 15, which matches the l^∞ case. But is that the minimal?

Wait, suppose in some Banach space, the norm is such that the coordinate functionals have smaller norms. For example, if the norm is defined such that ||α₁e₁ + α₂e₂ + α₃e₃|| = |α₁| + |α₂| + |α₃|. Then, the dual norm for each f_i would be 1, because in l^1, the dual is l^infty, so each coordinate functional has norm 1. Therefore, in this case, the sum would be 5*(1 +1 +1)=15. If the norm is l^infty, then the coordinate functionals have norm 1, sum is 15 again. In a Hilbert space with orthonormal basis, same thing. So regardless of the space, if the basis is normalized, the sum is 15. But if the basis vectors have different norms, then the dual norms change. For example, if e₁ has a very small norm, then ||f₁|| would be very large. Conversely, if e₁ has a large norm, ||f₁|| is smaller.

But the problem states "linearly independent vectors", but their norms are not specified. So, is it possible that in some Banach space, we can have the coordinate functionals with smaller norms, leading to a smaller sum?

Suppose we define a norm on ℝ³ such that the unit ball is the set of vectors where |α₁| + |α₂| + |α₃| ≤1. Then, the dual norm is the max norm. So the coordinate functionals have dual norm 1. So, in this case, the sum is 15. If we define a different norm, like ||v|| = sqrt(α₁² + α₂² + α₃²), which is the Euclidean norm, again the dual norms are 1. So, it seems that regardless of the norm, if the vectors e₁, e₂, e₃ are normalized, then the dual norms are 1. But the problem doesn't specify that the vectors are normalized. So, if the vectors have different norms, the dual functionals can have different norms.

Wait, let's take a specific example. Suppose in a Banach space E, the vectors e₁, e₂, e₃ are such that ||e₁||=1, ||e₂||=1, ||e₃||=1. Then, the norm of the linear combination α₁e₁ + α₂e₂ + α₃e₃ depends on the Banach space's norm. In l^1, the norm is sum |α_i|; in l^2, it's Euclidean; in l^infty, it's max |α_i|. But the dual norms of the coordinate functionals in each case are 1 for l^1 and l^infty, and 1 for l^2. So the sum is 15. But if the vectors are scaled differently. Suppose e₁ is (1,0,0), e₂ is (0,1,0), e₃ is (0,0,1) in l^1 space. Then, ||e_i||=1, and the dual norm ||f_i||=1. If we scale e_i to have norm 2, then the dual norm becomes 0.5, so the |a_i| would be 5*0.5=2.5, so the sum would be 7.5. But then, the vectors are linearly independent but not normalized. However, the problem does not specify any restriction on the vectors other than being linearly independent. So, in this case, could we have a Banach space where the coordinate functionals have arbitrarily small norms, leading to arbitrarily small sum |a₁| + |a₂| + |a₃|?

Wait, but if we scale the vectors e_i to have very large norms, then the dual functionals would have very small norms, and thus the |a_i|=5||f_i|| would be very small. But then, the linear combination α₁e₁ + ... with ||v|| ≤5 would require the coefficients α_i to be scaled down. For example, suppose e_i has norm M, then to have ||α₁e₁ + α₂e₂ + α₃e₃|| ≤5, each |α_i| must be ≤5/M. Then, the coordinate functional norms would be 1/M, so |a_i| =5*(1/M). But if M is made larger, the |a_i| can be made smaller. But since the problem doesn't restrict the norms of e_i, can we make M as large as possible, leading to |a_i| approaching zero? That seems possible.

But the problem says "linearly independent vectors", not necessarily normalized. So, technically, in such a case, if we take e₁, e₂, e₃ with huge norms, then the coefficients α_i would have to be tiny to keep the norm of the combination ≤5, hence the |a_i| could be made very small. However, the problem asks for the minimal possible sum |a₁| + |a₂| + |a₃| such that the bounds hold. So, theoretically, there's no lower bound, we can make the sum as small as we like by scaling up the vectors e_i. But that contradicts the problem's requirement to find the smallest possible value. Therefore, I must have made a mistake in my reasoning.

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 1.3 Problem Explanation
The issue describes problems with the `DatePicker` component when used in "menu" form:
1. **Min/Max value setting**: Users cannot directly set `min_` and `max_` values; they must navigate through `datepicker.children.....min_` which is inconvenient
2. **Default value initialization**: The `v_model` parameter for setting initial values is hardcoded, preventing proper initialization
3. **Value linking**: The `jslink` mechanism only works for JavaScript-triggered changes, not for linking values from initialization

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Create a DatePicker in menu form
2. Try to set min_/max_ values directly - should fail or be cumbersome
3. Try to set initial v_model value - should be ignored due to hardcoding
4. Try to link values using jslink from initialization - should not work properly

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Components should expose commonly used parameters directly
- Default values should be configurable, not hardcoded
- Value linking should work from initialization, not just runtime changes

    27	   <#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256
#define WARP_SIZE 32

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2)
        val = max(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));
    return val;
}

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2)
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    return val;
}

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int lane_id = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    int warps_per_block = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;
    
    extern __shared__ float shared[];
    float* warp_max = shared;
    float* warp_sum = &shared[warps_per_block];

    // Initialize thread local max
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    
    // Handle large feature counts with strided access
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }
    
    // Warp-level reduction for max
    float warp_max_val = warp_reduce_max(thread_max);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_max[warp_id] = warp_max_val;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < warps_per_block) {
        float block_max = warp_max[lane_id];
        block_max = warp_reduce_max(block_max);
        warp_max[0] = block_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    float max_val = warp_max[0];
    
    // Compute exp and sum
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    
    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }
    
    // Warp-level reduction for sum
    float warp_sum_val = warp_reduce_sum(thread_sum);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_sum[warp_id] = warp_sum_val;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < warps_per_block) {
        float block_sum = warp_sum[lane_id];
        block_sum = warp_reduce_sum(block_sum);
        warp_sum[0] = block_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    float sum_val = warp_sum[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;
    
    // Normalize with strided access
    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    
    int warps_per_block = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * warps_per_block * 2;
    
    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32");
    
    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the smallest possible value of \( |a_1| + |a_2| + |a_3| \) such that for all \((\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \alpha_3) \in S(5)\), the inequalities \( |\alpha_1| \leq |a_1| \), \( |\alpha_2| \leq |a_2| \), and \( |\alpha_3| \leq |a_3| \) hold. Here, \( S(5) \) is defined as the set of all linear combinations \( \alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3 \) with norm at most 5, where \( e_1, e_2, e_3 \) are linearly independent vectors in a real Banach space \( E \).

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Understanding the Problem:**
   - We need to find bounds \( |a_1|, |a_2|, |a_3| \) such that for any vector in \( S(5) \), the coefficients \( \alpha_i \) are bounded by \( |a_i| \).
   - The set \( S(5) \) is defined as \( \{ (\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \alpha_3) \mid \|\alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3\| \leq 5 \} \).

2. **Coordinate Functionals:**
   - Consider the coordinate functionals \( f_i \) defined by \( f_i(\alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3) = \alpha_i \).
   - The norm of each coordinate functional \( f_i \) is given by \( \|f_i\| = \sup \left\{ \frac{|\alpha_i|}{\|\alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3\|} \mid \alpha_1, \alpha_2, \alpha_3 \in \mathbb{R} \right\} \).

3. **Bounding the Coefficients:**
   - For any vector \( \alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3 \) in \( S(5) \), we have \( \|\alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3\| \leq 5 \).
   - Therefore, \( |\alpha_i| \leq \|f_i\| \cdot \|\alpha_1 e_1 + \alpha_2 e_2 + \alpha_3 e_3\| \leq 5 \|f_i\| \).

4. **Finding the Minimal Sum:**
   - To ensure \( |\alpha_i| \leq |a_i| \) for all \( (\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \alpha_3) \in S(5) \), we set \( |a_i| = 5 \|f_i\| \).
   - The minimal sum \( |a_1| + |a_2| + |a_3| \) is then \( 5(\|f_1\| + \|f_2\| + \|f_3\|) \).

5. **Norms of Coordinate Functionals:**
   - In common Banach spaces such as \( \ell^1 \), \( \ell^2 \), or \( \ell^\infty \), the norms of the coordinate functionals are typically 1.
   - Therefore, \( \|f_1\| = \|f_2\| = \|f_3\| = 1 \).

6. **Conclusion:**
   - The minimal sum \( |a_1| + |a_2| + |a_3| \) is \( 5(1 + 1 + 1) = 15 \).

    <#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int stride = blockDim.x;

    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    extern __shared__ float sdata[];
    float* max_shared = sdata;
    float* sum_shared = &sdata[stride];

    // Find max value using thread-local variable first
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }

    // Store thread-local max in shared memory
    max_shared[tid] = thread_max;
    __syncthreads();

    // Parallel reduction for max
    for (int offset = stride/2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {
        if (tid < offset) {
            max_shared[tid] = max(max_shared[tid], max_shared[tid + offset]);
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float max_val = max_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Compute exponentials and partial sums in thread-local variable
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;  // Store intermediate result
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }

    // Store thread-local sum
    sum_shared[tid] = thread_sum;
    __syncthreads();

    // Parallel reduction for sum
    for (int offset = stride/2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {
        if (tid < offset) {
            sum_shared[tid] += sum_shared[tid + offset];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float sum_val = sum_shared[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;

    // Final normalization without atomic operations
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);

    // Double the shared memory to store both max and sum values
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

// CUDA kernel for softmax using stride loops and proper boundary handling
__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    // Each block processes one row of the input (batch element)
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int blockSize = blockDim.x;

    // Pointers to the start of the current row
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    // Allocate shared memory: first half for max reduction, second half for sum reduction
    extern __shared__ float shared_mem[];
    float* max_shared = shared_mem;
    float* sum_shared = shared_mem + blockSize;

    // Step 1: Compute the maximum value in the row using stride loop
    float max_val_local = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        float val = x_row[i];
        if (val > max_val_local) {
            max_val_local = val;
        }
    }
    max_shared[tid] = max_val_local;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the maximum value in shared memory
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            if (max_shared[tid + s] > max_shared[tid]) {
                max_shared[tid] = max_shared[tid + s];
            }
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float row_max = max_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 2: Compute exponentials and partial sum using stride loop
    float sum_val_local = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - row_max);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;  // Store the intermediate exponential result
        sum_val_local += exp_val;
    }
    sum_shared[tid] = sum_val_local;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the sum of exponentials
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            sum_shared[tid] += sum_shared[tid + s];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float total_sum = sum_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 3: Normalize the results
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        y_row[i] = y_row[i] / total_sum;
    }
}

// CUDA forward function
void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);

    // Allocate shared memory for both max and sum reductions
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

// C++ forward function exposed to PyTorch
torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    int batch_size = x.size(0);
    int num_features = x.size(1);

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(x.data_ptr<float>(), y.data_ptr<float>(), batch_size, num_features);

    return y;
}

// Pybind11 module
PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");
}

Bernoulli numbers... Bernoulli's formula... Yes, the formula is:

S_k(n) = (1/(k+1)) * sum_{m=0}^k (k+1 choose m) B_m n^{k+1 - m}

S_1(n) = (1/2)n^2 + (1/2)n, which matches n(n+1)/2. 

For k=2:

S_2(n) = (1/3)n^3 + (1/2)n^2 + (1/6)n. Factoring that gives n(n+1)(2n+1)/6. So it works.

So in general, the sum S_k(n) is a polynomial of degree k+1 where the coefficients involve Bernoulli numbers. But maybe the question expects the general formula using Bernoulli numbers?

Let me outline a possible algorithm in steps:

1. For each number in the sequence:

   a. Compute max digit and thus the min base. 

   b. If any of the numbers has a min_base >10, return 0.

2. If all are okay, then:

   a. Find all possible combinations of bases (for each number within their min..10).

   b. For each such combination, check:

      i. Convert each number to decimal using its base. Call the resulting array of decimal values D = d1, d2, ..., dn.

      ii. Check if D is strictly increasing. 

      iii. If so, compute the sum of the chosen bases.

      iv. Track the maximum such sum, and when all are checked, return the maximum. 

   c. If no such combination is found, return 0.

  /testbed/sepal_ui/se<#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256
#define WARP_SIZE 32

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val = max(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));
    }
    return val;
}

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    }
    return val;
}

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int lane_id = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;

    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    extern __shared__ float shared[];
    float* warp_maxes = shared;
    float* warp_sums = &shared[num_warps];

    // Find max value using thread-local variable first
    float thread_max = -FLT_MAX;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }

    // Warp-level reduction for max
    float warp_max = warp_reduce_max(thread_max);

    // Store warp results in shared memory
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_maxes[warp_id] = warp_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < num_warps) {
        float final_max = warp_maxes[lane_id];
        final_max = warp_reduce_max(final_max);
        warp_maxes[0] = final_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    float max_val = warp_maxes[0];

    // Compute exponentials and partial sums
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }

    // Warp-level reduction for sum
    float warp_sum = warp_reduce_sum(thread_sum);

    // Store warp results
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_sums[warp_id] = warp_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < num_warps) {
        float final_sum = warp_sums[lane_id];
        final_sum = warp_reduce_sum(final_sum);
        warp_sums[0] = final_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    float sum_val = warp_sums[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;

    // Final normalization
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);

    int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * num_warps * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");
}<#sep#>oulli numbers with B_1 = +1/2. Therefore, the formula is correct when using the Bernoulli numbers where B_1 = +1/2. So the formula is:

sum_{i=1}^n i^k = (1/(k+1)) sum_{m=0}^k binom(k+1, m) B_m n^{k+1 - m}

where B_0=1, B_1=1/2, B_2=1/6, B_3=0, B_4=-1/30, etc. So the formula holds with these Bernoulli numbers.

So the answer is that the sum is equal to (1/(k+1)) times the sum from m=0 to k of (k+1 choose m) times B_m times n^{k+1 - m}, where B_m are Bernoulli numbers with B_1 = +1/2.

Hence, the boxed answer would be:

\boxed{\frac{1}{k+1} \sum_{m=0}^k \binom{k+1}{m} B_m n^{k+1 - m}}

Alternatively, since Bernoulli numbers can be represented as B_m, and the problem might expect this general formula. So this is the standard formula for the sum, so this should be the answer.
</think>To find the sum \( \sum_{i=1}^n i^k \), we use Faulhaber's formula, which expresses the sum of the \( k \)-th powers of the first \( n \) natural numbers in terms of Bernoulli numbers \( B_m \). The formula is:

\[
\sum_{i=1}^n i^k = \frac{1}{k+1} \sum_{m=0}^k \binom{k+1}{m} B_m n^{k+1 - m}
\]

Here, \( B_m \) are the Bernoulli numbers, where \( B_0 = 1 \), \( B_1 = \frac{1}{2} \), \( B_2 = \frac{1}{6} \), \( B_3 = 0 \), \( B_4 = -\frac{1}{30} \), and so on.

To illustrate, let's consider a few specific cases:

1. For \( k = 1 \):
   \[
   \sum_{i=1}^n i = \frac{1}{2} \left( \binom{2}{0} B_0 n^2 + \binom{2}{1} B_1 n^1 \right) = \frac{1}{2} \left( 1 \cdot 1 \cdot n^2 + 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \cdot n \right) = \frac{1}{2} \left( n^2 + n \right) = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}
   \]

2. For \( k = 2 \):
   \[
   \sum_{i=1}^n i^2 = \frac{1}{3} \left( \binom{3}{0} B_0 n^3 + \binom{3}{1} B_1 n^2 + \binom{3}{2} B_2 n^1 \right) = \frac{1}{3} \left( 1 \cdot 1 \cdot n^3 + 3 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \cdot n^2 + 3 \cdot \frac{1}{6} \cdot n \right) = \frac{1}{3} \left( n^3 + \frac{3}{2} n^2 + \frac{1}{2} n \right) = \frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
   \]

3. For \( k = 3 \):
   \[
   \sum_{i=1}^n i^3 = \frac{1}{4} \left( \binom{4}{0} B_0 n^4 + \binom{4}{1} B_1 n^3 + \binom{4}{2} B_2 n^2 + \binom{4}{3} B_3 n^1 \right) = \frac{1}{4} \left( 1 \cdot 1 \cdot n^4 + 4 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \cdot n^3 + 6 \cdot \frac{1}{6} \cdot n^2 + 4 \cdot 0 \cdot n \right) = \frac{1}{4} \left( n^4 + 2 n^3 + n^2 \right) = \left( \frac{n(n+1)}{2} \right)^2
   \]

In general, the formula for the sum of the \( k \)-th powers of the first \( n \) natural numbers is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{k+1} \sum_{m=0}^k \binom{k+1}{m} B_m n^{k+1 - m}}
\]

where \( B_m \) are the Bernoulli numbers with \( B_1 = \frac{1}{2} \).<#sep#> that programming problems might expect such code. Let me think of how to code this.

Alternative approaches include the following steps.

First, precompute for each number the possible bases and sort them in descending order so that when we iterate, higher bases are considered first, leading to early promising solutions and possibly early pruning of paths.

But even with that, trying all possible combinations might be feasible. Let's see, in Python, loops with 10**10 is impossible but for small amounts like 15 bases, it's okay. Let's consider:

Suppose each number has 10 possible bases (the maximum is if min base is 2, so 9 options (2-10) so up to 9 per number). So 4 numbers give 9^4 = ~6561 combinations, which is okay. 5 numbers would be 59049, still manageable. 

Assuming that in the problem's constraints, the numbers could be up to say, 8 terms, this might take around a million (so 1e9 is too much, but 1e6 is okay per Python's speed? Maybe in code golf, it might be manageable with loops.)

Therefore, perhaps this is a viable approach.

Now, how to implement this?

First, for each number, we precompute the list of possible bases in order descending (from 10 down to min_base). 

Then, we can loop through all possible bases for each position, and track the current maximum.

Alternatively, we can use a recursive approach with backtracking to try bases in descending order, keeping track of the current sum and the last converted number's value, and at each step pruning branches that can't exceed the current maximum.

This is likely more efficient as we can stop processing branches early when the path can't possibly yield a better sum than the current best.

The steps for backtracking would be like:

We need to process numbers one by one. 

Parameters for backtrack function: 

- current index of the number being considered (e.g., start with index 0)

- previous value (must be less than the current number's value)

- accumulated sum of bases so far.

- current best sum found.

The function would, for each possible base of current number (minimum to max, starting from higher bases first), compute the value of this number with that base, compare to previous:

- if current number's value > previous, then proceed to next numbers with the new base and updated sum and prev value.

- else, skip this base.

We can track the maximum sum as we go.

This way, if at any step, the remaining possible bases don't allow the current path's sum + the maximum possible bases of remaining numbers to exceed the current best, then this path can be pruned.

For example, the path's current sum is S. Suppose we're at the ith number, and there are (n - i) numbers left. The remaining maximum possible bases for each of those numbers is 10. So their contribution is at most (n - i)*10. Then, the total possible sum from this path is S + (n-i)*10. If this is less than the current best, there's no point proceeding down this path.

Also, since trying the largest bases first (for current number), if a path already reaches the current best, other paths with smaller bases (for this step) can't improve further, so we can break early for those options.

Therefore, the algorithm steps are:

Initialize best_sum = 0. 

Sort the possible bases for each number in descending order (so we try highest bases first). 

The base for each number must be between min_base_i and 10, and so for each number's bases we can have a list like [10, 9, ..., min_base].

We can process the numbers in order, for each one trying its possible bases in descending order. 

Here's the function outline:

def backtrack(index, current_sum, previous_value):

   global best_sum

   if index == number_of_numbers:

       if current_sum > best_sum: 

           best_sum = current_sum

       return

   for base in possible_bases[index] in descending order:

       if base < min_base[index]: continue  # not needed if list is precomputed properly.

       current_num_value = convert(number[index], base)

       if current_num_value <= previous_value: continue

       # proceed, also note that the sum can potentially improve.

       remaining_bases_max = (number_of_numbers - (index+1)) * 10

       # the possible max added is (10 for remaining numbers). 

       # current is current_sum + base + remaining_bases_max

       if current_sum + base + remaining_bases_max <= best_sum: 

           # even best case, can’t improve, skip all lower bases.

           break  # since they are in descending order.

       elif current_sum + base > best_sum:

           # might improve, proceed

           backtrack(index +1, current_sum + base, current_num_value)

   return

This way, when considering a base for the current step, the loop processes bases in descending order. We also check if the current chosen base allows, in the best case, to improve beyond the current best. If even the best case (assuming all remaining steps choose the max base 10) can't surpass the current best, then we break out of the loop, since the next bases (smaller) would only contribute less.

This dramatically prunes the search space.

This approach can be efficient even for longer sequences. Let's see for a 5number sequence: each step at least maybe 7 bases, but with pruning, the steps are manageable.

Thus, the algorithm can proceed as such.

So first step: read each number as a string, compute min base.

Then, check if the min_base exceeds 10 for any number → return 0.

Else, precompute for each number its possible bases in descending order (from 10 down to its min_base). 

Then initialize best_sum=0, and call backtrack function starting from 0 (index 0, current_sum 0, previous_value -infinity or a thing that ensures the first number is considered). Because for the first number, its value just needs to be positive (since non-negative and strictly increasing implies each term has to be less than next → first term must be less than second, etc. So previous_value can be initialized as -infinity (so that the first term can have any value as long is is positive, but wait first number can be zero? The problem says non-negative integers. So possible.

Wait let me think first number could be 0, but need to check that the sequence is strictly increasing. 

So, in the backtrack function's initial step, the first number can be any allowed base's value (non-negative) and then the second must be higher, etc.

In the first step, the previous_value is set to -infinity, so that the first term's value can be anything (but must be less than the second, but first's value is the first value, then previous is that, etc. So initial previous value can be -infinity (or negative number).

Thus, the algorithm steps:

Now, in code, let's try to build this.

Suppose the numbers are stored as a list like ['12', '11', '10'] in the sample.

Function to convert a string number to a list of digits would help. e.g.:

def get_min_base(number_str):

    return max_digit +1

#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256
#define WARP_SIZE 32

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2)
        val = max(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));
    return val;
}

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2)
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    return val;
}

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int lane_id = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    int warps_per_block = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;
    
    extern __shared__ float shared[];
    float* warp_max = shared;
    float* warp_sum = &shared[warps_per_block];

    // Initialize thread local max
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    
    // Handle large feature counts with strided access
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }
    
    // Warp-level reduction for max
    float warp_max_val = warp_reduce_max(thread_max);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_max[warp_id] = warp_max_val;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < warps_per_block) {
        float block_max = warp_max[lane_id];
        block_max = warp_reduce_max(block_max);
        warp_max[0] = block_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    float max_val = warp_max[0];
    
    // Compute exp and sum
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    
        for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockDim.x) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }
    
    // Warp-level reduction for sum
    float warp_sum_val = warp_reduce_sum(thread_sum);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_sum[warp_id] = warp_sum_val;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    // Final reduction across warps
    if (warp_id == 0 && lane_id < warps_per_block) {
        float block_sum = warp_sum[lane_id];
        block_sum = warp_reduce_sum(block_sum);
        warp_sum[0] = block_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    float sum_val = warp_sum[0];
    float inv_sum = 1.0f / sum_val;
    
    // Normalize with strided access
        for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockDim.x) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    
    int warps_per_block = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * warps_per_block * 2;
    
    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32");
    
    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

For each base in possible_bases[0] (first number's possible bases in descending):

compute the current value (d1).

If this is > prev_val (which it is, since -infinity → yes. Then the next steps are to proceed to next indexes, and so on.

But we need a way to compute the value of the number in a given base.

Function to convert a number string in base b to integer is needed.

def convert_num(s, base):

    res=0

    for c in s:

        d = int(c)

        res = res*base +d

    return res

That's a standard base conversion function. For example, '12' in base 6 is 1*6 +2=8.

So putting this together.

We can precompute for each number the list of possible bases, in reverse order (descending).

We can implement this using recursion with memoization, but given that in Python, tail recursion is not optimized, here's a possible approach using a recursive function with the pruning steps.

Now, the main outline:

def solve(numbers):

    n = len(numbers)

    # precompute min_bases for each number:

    min_bases = []

    for num_str in numbers:

        max_digit = max(int(c) for c in num_str)

        min_b = max_digit +1

        if min_b>10:

            return 0

        min_bases.append(min_b)

    # for each number i, possible bases are from min_base up to 10, in descending order:

    bases_lists = []

    for b in min_bases:

        bases = list(range(10, b-1, -1))

        bases_lists.append(bases)

    best_sum = 0

    # Define backtracking recursively. Since Python has stack limits, but for small n, okay.

    # use nested functions.

    def backtrack(index, current_sum, prev_val):

        nonlocal best_sum, bases_lists, numbers, n

        if index ==n:

            if current_sum>best_sum:

                best_sum= current_sum

            return 

        # current number is index

        bases = bases_lists[index]

        num_str = numbers[index]

        for base in bases:

            val = convert(num_str, base)

            if val <= prev_val:

                continue

            remaining = n -1 - index

            current_potential = current_sum + base + remaining *10

            if current_potential <= best_sum:

                break # no improvement possible

            next_sum = current_sum + base

            backtrack( index+1, next_sum, val)

    # initial call: first number index 0, sum 0, prev_val = -infinity (or -1)

    backtrack(0,0, -float('inf'))

    return best_sum if best_sum>0 else 0

Wait but in the initial call, prev_val must be -infinity (or a number lower than any possible first value. so that the first value can be anything). In code, perhaps use -infinity.

Now , need to implement the convert function:

def convert(num_str, base):

    res =0

    for c in num_str:

        d = int(c)

        res = res *base +d

    return res

Putting it all together.

But there's a small problem: The first number can be zero, but the sequence has to be strictly increasing. For example, if first number in base 2 is '0', then next number must be >0. But this is allowed, so the function should handle that.

#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <vector>

// Templated softmax kernel that allows tuning the block size at compile time
template <int BLOCK_SIZE>
__global__ void softmax_kernel_template(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    const int blockSize = BLOCK_SIZE;

    // Pointers to the start of the current row
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    // Allocate shared memory: first blockSize for max reduction, next blockSize for sum reduction
    extern __shared__ float shared_mem[];
    float* max_shared = shared_mem;
    float* sum_shared = shared_mem + blockSize;

    // Step 1: Compute the maximum value in the row using a stride loop
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }
    max_shared[tid] = thread_max;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the max value
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            max_shared[tid] = fmaxf(max_shared[tid], max_shared[tid + s]);
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float row_max = max_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 2: Compute exponentials and accumulate partial sums
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - row_max);
        y_row[i] = exp_val; // store intermediate result
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }
    sum_shared[tid] = thread_sum;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the sum of exponentials
    for (int s = blockSize / 2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            sum_shared[tid] += sum_shared[tid + s];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }

    float sum_val = sum_shared[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 3: Normalize the results
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += blockSize) {
        y_row[i] /= sum_val;
    }
}

// Host function to launch the kernel with a tunable block size
void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features, int block_size) {
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * block_size * 2; // for max and sum arrays

    switch(block_size) {
        case 32: {
            dim3 block_dim(32);
            softmax_kernel_template<32><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
        case 64: {
            dim3 block_dim(64);
            softmax_kernel_template<64><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
        case 128: {
            dim3 block_dim(128);
            softmax_kernel_template<128><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
        case 256: {
            dim3 block_dim(256);
            softmax_kernel_template<256><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
        case 512: {
            dim3 block_dim(512);
            softmax_kernel_template<512><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
        default: {
            // Default to 256 if an unsupported block size is provided
            dim3 block_dim(256);
            softmax_kernel_template<256><<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
            break;
        }
    }
}

// C++ forward function exposed to PyTorch
// Added optional parameter 'block_size' to allow experimentation with different configurations
torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x, int block_size = 256) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    int batch_size = x.size(0);
    int num_features = x.size(1);

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(x.data_ptr<float>(), y.data_ptr<float>(), batch_size, num_features, block_size);
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA) with tunable block size",
          py::arg("x"), py::arg("block_size") = 256);
}
<#sep#> 3*sqrt(2/3) = sqrt(6) ≈ 2.449, which is higher than 2. So that's higher.

Alternatively, suppose one variable is 1, and the other two variables are equal. Let's say x = 1, y = z. Then x² + 2y² = 2 => 1 + 2y² = 2 => y² = 0.5 => y = sqrt(0.5) ≈ 0.707. Then sum is 1 + 2*0.707 ≈ 2.414, still higher than 2.

Alternatively, maybe two variables at 1 and the third at 0 gives sum 2, which seems better.

Wait, but can we get even lower? For example, if two variables are 1, but the third is allowed to be negative. But no, variables are in [0,1], so that's not possible.

Alternatively, if only one variable is 1, and the other two are between 0 and 1. As we saw earlier, the sum is higher than 2.

Alternatively, if none of the variables are 1. Let's try, for example, two variables at a, and one at b. So 2a² + b² = 2. We want to minimize 2a + b. Let's set up Lagrangian:

L = 2a + b + λ(2a² + b² - 2)

Take derivatives with respect to a, b, λ.

dL/da = 2 + 4λa = 0 => λ = -1/(2a)

dL/db = 1 + 2λb = 0 => λ = -1/(2b)

Setting these equal: -1/(2a) = -1/(2b) => a = b.

But then, since a = b, the original equation would be 2a² + a² = 3a² = 2 => a = sqrt(2/3) ≈ 0.816. Then sum is 2a + a = 3a ≈ 2.449, which is sqrt(6) again, which is the same as the case when all variables are equal. So that's not better than 2.

Alternatively, if we take different numbers. Suppose one variable is 1, another is 1, and the third is 0. Sum is 2, which is lower.

Alternatively, suppose one variable is 1, another is t, and the third is s. Then 1 + t² + s² = 2, so t² + s² =1. To minimize 1 + t + s. The minimum of t + s with t² + s² =1 and t, s ∈ [0,1]. The minimum occurs when one is 1 and the other is 0. So t=1, s=0, sum 1 + 1 + 0 = 2. So same as before.

Hence, in all cases, the minimum sum seems to be 2. So the answer should be 2.

But let me check another approach. Let me consider using Lagrange multipliers for the problem.

We need to minimize f(α, β, γ) = cos α + cos β + cos γ, subject to the constraint g(α, β, γ) = sin²α + sin²β + sin²γ - 1 = 0.

Set up the Lagrangian: L = cos α + cos β + cos γ + λ(sin²α + sin²β + sin²γ - 1)

Take partial derivatives:

∂L/∂α = -sin α + λ*2 sin α cos α = 0

Similarly for β and γ:

∂L/∂β = -sin β + λ*2 sin β cos β = 0

∂L/∂γ = -sin γ + λ*2 sin γ cos γ = 0

So, for each angle, the equation is:

-sin θ + 2λ sin θ cos θ = 0, where θ is α, β, γ.

Assuming sin θ ≠ 0 (since if sin θ =0, then θ=0, cos θ=1. Let's check that case later.)

For sin θ ≠ 0, we can divide by sin θ:

-1 + 2λ cos θ = 0 => 2λ cos θ = 1 => λ = 1/(2 cos θ)

But this must hold for all θ where sin θ ≠0. So either all the angles have the same cos θ, meaning α = β = γ, or some angles are 0 (with sin θ =0).

If all angles are equal, then α = β = γ. Then sin²α + sin²β + sin²γ = 3 sin²α =1 => sin²α =1/3 => sin α = 1/√3, so α = arcsin(1/√3). Then cos α = sqrt(1 - 1/3) = sqrt(2/3). So sum cos α + cos β + cos γ = 3*sqrt(2/3) = sqrt(6) ≈ 2.449.

Alternatively, if some angles are 0. Let's say α = 0, then sin α =0, cos α =1. The constraint becomes 0 + sin²β + sin²γ =1. So sin²β + sin²γ =1. Then the minimum of cos β + cos γ +1. We need to minimize cos β + cos γ given sin²β + sin²γ =1.

Again, similar to before. If β and γ are both π/4, then sin²β = sin²γ =1/2, sum 1. Then cos β + cos γ = 2*(sqrt(2)/2) = sqrt(2) ≈1.414. So total sum is 1 + sqrt(2) ≈2.414, which is more than 2. But if one of β or γ is π/2, say β = π/2, then sin²β =1, sin²γ=0. Then γ =0, cos γ=1. Then sum is 1 (from α=0) + 0 (from β=π/2) +1 (from γ=0) =2. Which is the same as the previous case where two angles are 0 and one is π/2.

Wait, but in this case, α=0, β=π/2, γ=0. Then sin²α + sin²β + sin²γ =0 +1 +0=1. So that satisfies the constraint. Then cos sum is 1 +0 +1=2.

Hence, when two angles are 0 (cos=1), and one is π/2 (cos=0), sum is 2. Alternatively, when two angles are π/2 and one is 0? Wait, no, if two angles are π/2, then sin² each is 1, so total sin² sum would be 2, which is more than 1. So that's not allowed.

So the case where two angles are 0 (so sin^2=0) and one angle is π/2 (sin^2=1) gives the sum of 1 +1 +0=2. Therefore, that seems to be the minimum.

In the Lagrangian approach, the critical points are either all angles equal (leading to sum sqrt(6)) or some angles at the boundaries (i.e., 0 or π/2). The boundary case gives the lower sum. Therefore, the minimum is indeed 2.

Therefore, the minimum possible value is 2.

**Final Answer**

\]

\[

min_bases:

First number 12's digits:1 and 2 → max 2 → min_base 3.

Bases_lists for first would be 10 down to 3 → [10,9,8,7,6,5,4,3]

popped v_model: 2022-03-14
kwargs2 after pop<#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256
#define WARP_SIZE 32

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val = max(val, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));
    }
    return val;
}

__inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = WARP_SIZE/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) {
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    }
    return val;
}

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    const int tid = threadIdx.x;
    const int lane_id = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    const int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    const int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    const int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;
    
    extern __shared__ float shared_data[];
    float* warp_maxes = shared_data;
    float* warp_sums = shared_data + num_warps;

    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    
    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }

    float warp_max = warp_reduce_max(thread_max);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_maxes[warp_id] = warp_max;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    if (warp_id == 0) {
        float val = (tid < num_warps) ? warp_maxes[tid] : -INFINITY;
        val = warp_reduce_max(val);
        if (tid == 0) {
            warp_maxes[0] = val;
        }
    }
    __syncthreads();

    const float row_max = warp_maxes[0];
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;

    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - row_max);
        y_row[i] = exp_val;
        thread_sum += exp_val;
    }

    float warp_sum = warp_reduce_sum(thread_sum);
    
    if (lane_id == 0) {
        warp_sums[warp_id] = warp_sum;
    }
    __syncthreads();

    if (warp_id == 0) {
        float val = (tid < num_warps) ? warp_sums[tid] : 0.0f;
        val = warp_reduce_sum(val);
        if (tid == 0) {
            warp_sums[0] = val;
        }
    }
    __syncthreads();

    const float inv_sum = 1.0f / warp_sums[0];

    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += THREADS_PER_BLOCK) {
        y_row[i] *= inv_sum;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    
    int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * num_warps * 2;

    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32");

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");
}<#sep#> \theta\) to rewrite the given condition:
\[
\sin^2 \alpha + \sin^2 \beta + \sin^2 \gamma = 1 \implies (1 - \cos^2 \alpha) + (1 - \cos^2 \beta) + (1 - \cos^2 \gamma) = 1.
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
3 - (\cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma) = 1 \implies \cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma = 2.
\]

We need to minimize \(\cos \alpha + \cos \beta + \cos \gamma\) under the constraint \(\cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma = 2\). Considering the angles \(\alpha, \beta, \gamma\) in the interval \([0, \frac{\pi}{2}]\), the cosines are non-negative.

Let's test the boundary values:
- Suppose \(\alpha = 0\), \(\beta = 0\), and \(\gamma = \frac{\pi}{2}\). Then:
  \[
  \sin^2 0 + \sin^2 0 + \sin^2 \frac{\pi}{2} = 0 + 0 + 1 = 1,
  \]
  and the sum of the cosines is:
  \[
  \cos 0 + \cos 0 + \cos \frac{\pi}{2} = 1 + 1 + 0 = 2.
  \]

To verify if this is indeed the minimum, we consider other possible configurations:
- If all angles are equal, \(\alpha = \beta = \gamma\). Then:
  \[
  \cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \alpha = 3 \cos^2 \alpha = 2 \implies \cos^2 \alpha = \frac{2}{3} \implies \cos \alpha = \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}.
  \]
  The sum of the cosines is:
  \[
  \cos \alpha + \cos \beta + \cos \gamma = 3 \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}} = \sqrt{6} \approx 2.449,
  \]
  which is greater than 2.

- If one angle is \(\frac{\pi}{2}\) and the other two angles are equal, say \(\alpha = \beta\) and \(\gamma = \frac{\pi}{2}\), then:
  \[
  \sin^2 \alpha + \sin^2 \alpha + \sin^2 \frac{\pi}{2} = 2 \sin^2 \alpha + 1 = 1 \implies \sin^2 \alpha = 0 \implies \alpha = 0.
  \]
  The sum of the cosines is:
  \[
  \cos 0 + \cos 0 + \cos \frac{\pi}{2} = 1 + 1 + 0 = 2.
  \]

Thus, the minimum possible value of \(\cos \alpha + \cos \beta + \cos \gamma\) is \(\boxed{2}\).<#sep#> → minimal base 2. So in base 2 gives 1 → so now sequence is 0 <1 <2.

Alright, now, the modified code should be:

So, the final code in Python would be:

def solve(numbers):

    def get_min_base(s):

        digits = [int(c) for c in s]

        max_d = max(digits)

        min_b = max_d + 1

        min_b = max(min_b, 2)  # since minimal base is 2

        return min_b

    n = len(numbers)

    min_bases = []

    for num_str in numbers:

        min_b = get_min_base(num_str)

        if min_b>10:

            return 0

        min_bases.append(min_b)

    # create lists of possible bases for each number in descending order:

### 5.1 Problem Statement
The DatePicker component has three main issues:
1. Missing `date_picker` attribute - users cannot access the underlying date picker directly
2. Layout kwargs not handled - `layout_kwargs` parameter is ignored
3. Parameter forwarding - `min`, `max` and other parameters are not forwarded to the underlying date picker

### 5.2 Problem Location
The problem is in `/testbed/sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/inputs.py` in the `DatePicker.__init__` method (lines 51-93).

### 5.3 Test Reproduction
The failing test `test_kwargs` in `/testbed/tests/test_DatePicker.py` reproduces the issue by expecting:
- `datepicker.date_picker.min` and `datepicker.date_picker.max` to be accessible
- Layout parameters from `layout_kwargs` to be applied

### 5.4 Best Practices
- Components should expose commonly used parameters directly
- Default values should be configurable, not hardcoded
- Parameter forwarding should be explicit and clear

### <#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

// CUDA kernel for softmax using stride loops to handle large workloads and correct boundary handling
__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    // Each block handles one row (batch element)
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int stride = blockDim.x;

    // Pointers for the current row
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;

    // Allocate shared memory: first part for max reduction, second for sum reduction
    extern __shared__ float shared[];
    float* s_max = shared;            // size: blockDim.x
    float* s_sum = shared + stride;     // size: blockDim.x

    // Step 1: compute local maximum using stride loop
    float local_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        float val = x_row[i];
        local_max = fmaxf(local_max, val);
    }
    s_max[tid] = local_max;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to find the maximum value in the row
    for (int s = stride >> 1; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            s_max[tid] = fmaxf(s_max[tid], s_max[tid + s]);
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }
    float max_val = s_max[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 2: compute exponentials and a partial sum using stride loop
    float local_sum = 0.0f;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        // Compute exponential in a numerically stable way
        float exp_val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = exp_val; // store intermediate result
        local_sum += exp_val;
    }
    s_sum[tid] = local_sum;
    __syncthreads();

    // Reduction to compute the total sum
    for (int s = stride >> 1; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            s_sum[tid] += s_sum[tid + s];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }
    float total_sum = s_sum[0];
    __syncthreads();

    // Step 3: normalize the results to get softmax output
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        y_row[i] = y_row[i] / total_sum;
    }
}

// CUDA forward function
void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    
    // Shared memory allocation: two arrays of size THREADS_PER_BLOCK (one for max and one for sum)
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK * 2;
    
    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

// C++ forward function exposed to PyTorch
torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");

    int batch_size = x.size(0);
    int num_features = x.size(1);

    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    softmax_forward_cuda(x.data_ptr<float>(), y.data_ptr<float>(), batch_size, num_features);
    return y;
}

// Pybind11 module
PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");
}

To solve this problem, we need to determine the bases for each number in a given sequence such that the sequence is strictly increasing when converted to decimal values. Our goal is to maximize the sum of these bases.

### Approach
1. **Determine Minimum Base for Each Number**: For each number, the minimum base it can be in is determined by its maximum digit plus 1. Since bases must be between 2 and 10, we ensure the minimum base is at least 2.
2. **Check Validity**: If any number has a minimum base greater than 10, it's impossible to form a valid sequence, so we return 0.
3. **Backtracking with Pruning**: We use a backtracking approach to explore all possible bases for each number, starting from the highest possible bases (to maximize the sum). 
   - We start from the highest possible base for each number.
   - Track the cumulative sum of bases and the previous number's decimal value to ensure the sequence is strictly increasing.
   - Use pruning to stop exploring paths where the remaining bases cannot improve the current best sum.

### Solution Code
```python
def solve(numbers):
    def get_min_base(s):
        digits = [int(c) for c in s]
        max_d = max(digits)
        min_b = max_d + 1
        # Base must be at least 2
        min_b = max(min_b, 2)
        return min_b

    n = len(numbers)
    min_bases = []
    possible = True

    for num_str in numbers:
        min_b = get_min_base(num_str)
        if min_b > 10:
            possible = False
            break
        min_bases.append(min_b)

    if not possible:
        return 0

    bases_lists = []
    for b in min_bases:
        bases = list(range(10, b - 1, -1))
        bases_lists.append(bases)

    best_sum = 0

    def convert(num_str, base):
        res = 0
        for c in num_str:
            res = res * base + int(c)
        return res

    def backtrack(index, current_sum, prev_val):
        nonlocal best_sum
        if index == n:
            if current_sum > best_sum:
                best_sum = current_sum
            return

        bases = bases_lists[index]
        num_str = numbers[index]

        for base in bases:
            val = convert(num_str, base)
            if val <= prev_val:
                continue

            remaining = n - index - 1
            potential = current_sum + base + remaining * 10
            if potential <= best_sum:
                break  # No need to check further, since bases are in descending order

            backtrack(index + 1, current_sum + base, val)

    backtrack(0, 0, -float('inf'))

    return best_sum if best_sum != 0 else 0

# Example usage:
# numbers = ['12', '11', '10']
# print(solve(numbers))  # Output should be 24

# Another test case
# print(solve(['0', '1', '2']))  # Should return 30
# print(solve(['9']))  # Should return 10
# print(solve(['102', '10000', '10']))  # Should return 0
```

### Explanation
1. **Minimum Base Calculation**: Each number's minimum base is calculated based on its digits. This ensures we only consider valid bases (2 to 10).
2. **Backtracking with Pruning**: By iterating through possible bases starting from the highest to the lowest, we prioritize higher bases to maximize the sum. Pruning is applied if the current path cannot yield a better result than the current best, significantly reducing the search space.
3. **Conversion and Validation**: For each base choice, the number is converted to a decimal value. The sequence is checked to ensure it remains strictly increasing. Only valid paths are pursued further, optimizing the solution efficiently.

This approach ensures we explore the most promising paths first, leading to an optimal solution while efficiently prunable paths that cannot improve the result.<#sep#>bed/sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/inputs.py` in the `DatePicker.__init__` method (lines 51-93).

### 5.3 Test Reproduction
The failing test `test_kwargs` in `/testbed/tests/test_DatePicker.py` reproduces the issue by expecting:
- `datepicker.date_picker.min` and `datepicker.date_picker.max` to be accessible
- Layout parameters from `layout_kwargs` to be applied

### 5.4 Best Practices
- Components should expose commonly used parameters directly
- Default values should be configurable, not hardcoded
- Parameter forwarding should be explicit and clear

### 5.5 Solution
1. Store the `date_picker` widget as `self.date_picker` instance attribute
2. Extract date picker parameters (like `min`, `max`) from kwargs and forward them
3. Handle `layout_kwargs` by extracting and applying them to the widget
4. Maintain backward compatibility
